They knew I was smart enough to answer questions when they got my attention, but I just had a hard time staying on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of being very restless, fidgety, interrupting others, or acting before thinking, or is it mainly trouble paying attention and staying organized?\n\nPatient: I do get quite restless inside and fidgety, like when I'm sitting in a long meeting or trying to read quietly. But I don't really have issues with interrupting people or acting impulsively. My biggest, most disruptive struggles are definitely with paying attention, getting sidetracked, and staying organized.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems and restlessness cause clear difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause problems in a few areas of my life. At home, my wife and kids are losing trust in me because I keep missing appointments and leaving household projects unfinished, which makes them worry about my memory. It also affects my volunteer work at the senior center, where I've been double-booking myself and letting commitments fall through.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention and organization problems happen in more than one setting, such as both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were very noticeable back then, though I mainly remember it being tied to school-related tasks and feedback. My teachers would constantly tell my parents that I was daydreaming and missing instructions in class, and then at home, I'd always realize I had forgotten to bring the right books back with me."
  }

(Losing_Items): I’m always walking around the house patting my pockets, asking my wife if she’s seen my glasses, keys, or phone, because I’ll set them down for a moment while I’m doing something and then have no idea where I left them.\n- S008 (Distractibility): I’ll go to put something in the shed or start cooking and then a bird outside the window, the TV in the other room, or just a passing meeting at the community center or sitting in the waiting room at the clinic, I feel restless inside, like I want to get up and move around, and I keep shifting in my seat and looking for an excuse to stand up or walk out to the hallway.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): I like the idea of just sitting and reading or doing quiet meditation like they offer at the senior center, but after a few Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. My family says I’m always getting sidetracked and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing. They've gotten really worried that it might be some kind of memory problem, so I agreed to come in and get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and forgetfulness problems start when you were a child or teenager, or did they begin only recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: Well, it has become much more noticeable over the past year since I retired and took on more responsibilities, but these issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in primary school, my teachers would tell my parents that I was always daydreaming, missing instructions, and forgetting my schoolbooks. They knew I was smart enough to answer questions when they got my attention, but I just had a hard time staying on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of being very restless, fidgety, interrupting others, or acting before thinking, or is it mainly trouble paying attention and staying organized?\n\nPatient: I do get quite restless inside and fidgety, like when I'm sitting in a long meeting or trying to read quietly. But I don't really have issues with interrupting people or acting impulsively. My biggest, most disruptive struggles are definitely with paying attention, getting sidetracked, and staying organized.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems and restlessness cause clear difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause problems in a few areas of my life. At home, my wife and kids are losing trust in me because I keep missing appointments and leaving household projects unfinished, which makes them worry about my memory. It also affects my volunteer work at the senior center, where I've been double-booking myself and letting commitments fall through.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention and organization problems happen in more than one setting, such as both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were very noticeable back then, though I mainly remember it being tied to school-related tasks and feedback. My teachers would constantly tell my parents that I was daydreaming and missing instructions in class, and then at home, I'd always realize I had forgotten to bring the right books back with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have clear hyperactive or impulsive behaviors, such as being unable to stay seated, talking excessively, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking?\n\nPatient: I don't really talk excessively or blurt things out, but I definitely struggle with sitting still. During long meetings or in waiting rooms, I feel a strong internal restlessness, constantly shifting in my seat and looking for excuses to stand up and walk around. Even when trying to read or do quiet activities, my legs get fidgety and I feel the urge to get up and tinker with something instead.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and restlessness symptoms cause clear problems at work, at home, or in other activities, and have they been present since childhood in more than one setting?"

My teachers would constantly tell my parents that I was daydreaming and missing instructions in class, and then at home, I'd always realize I had forgotten to bring the right books back with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have clear hyperactive or impulsive behaviors, such as being unable to stay seated, talking excessively, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking?\n\nPatient: I don't really talk excessively or blurt things out, but I definitely struggle with sitting still. During long meetings or in waiting rooms, I feel a strong internal restlessness, constantly shifting in my seat and looking for excuses to stand up and walk around. Even when trying to read or do quiet activities, my legs get fidgety and I feel the urge to get up and tinker with something instead.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and restlessness symptoms cause clear problems at work, at home, or in other activities, and have they been present since childhood in more than one setting?\n\nPatient: Yes, they cause real problems now, like double-booking myself, missing appointments, and letting down my family and the senior center, which has caused my wife and kids to lose some trust in me. This definitely goes back to my childhood too, as my teachers often told my parents I was daydreaming and missing instructions at school, and I'd constantly forget to bring my schoolbooks home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of head injury, stroke, seizures, depression, anxiety, sleep problems, or alcohol/drug use that could better explain these attention and restlessness symptoms?"

No, I have never had any head injuries, strokes, seizures, or struggles with depression or anxiety. My sleep is generally fine, and I don't have any issues with alcohol or drugs either.

My main issues are much more about getting sidetracked, losing my focus, and feeling that internal restlessness when I have to sit still.
